People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello, Dr. [Name]. I appreciate you taking the time to see me. It's just... well, it's been getting to the point where I don't know what else to do, you know? I came in because I feel like I just cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It's this awful, constant feeling. Like, even when I am just absolutely worn out—and at my age, I get worn out a lot quicker than I used to, let me tell you—I still feel like I have to jump up and do the next thing. I should be sitting still, maybe watching a little television or reading a magazine, resting my bones, but my body just won't let me. It's like there's a motor humming inside me that won't switch off, and it's exhausting, it really is. I try to sit in my favorite chair, the one by the window where I can see the birds, but within two minutes, I'm thinking about the dishes, or the laundry, or something out in the yard that doesn't even need doing. I just can't seem to find any peace or stillness, no matter how tired I am.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, medications... well, let me think about that for a second. I have one of those little plastic pill organizers, you know the ones? With the days of the week stamped on the lids, Sunday through Saturday. I have to use it because if I just leave the bottles in the cabinet, I'll open one up, set it on the counter, get distracted by a smudge on the bathroom mirror, and completely forget if I actually swallowed the pill or just thought about swallowing it! 

But as for anything new, or changing any doses... no. No, I haven't started anything new at all. Definitely nothing for my mood or for my stomach. Thank goodness I don't get nauseous, I've always had a cast-iron stomach, even when I was younger and eating all sorts of junk. I just take my regular daily vitamin, and my calcium for my bones, and the exact same blood pressure pill I’ve been on since right around the time I stopped working. Oh, heavens no. No weight loss here, let me tell you! I honestly wish I could say yes to that one, because I've been trying to drop about ten pounds since my niece's wedding back in—oh, when was that? 2018? 2019? Somewhere around there. But no, my weight has been exactly the same for years. I still bake my own bread, you see, and I just can't resist a warm slice with a little butter right out of the oven, so the scale hasn't budged one bit. 

And as for feeling hot or sweaty... well, no, not at all. If anything, I'm the exact opposite! I'm always walking around the house in a thick cardigan. I keep the thermostat set right at 71 degrees, which I think is perfectly reasonable, but my sister comes over and she says, "How can you stand it in here, it's freezing!" But I just tell her to bring a sweater. I don't sweat unless I'm out pulling weeds in the dead of July, and even then, I try to get all my yard work done before ten in the morning so I don't overheat. 

And my heart... let me think. No, no racing. Like I said before, I take my blood pressure pill every morning, and I actually check my pulse sometimes when I'm at the grocery store waiting for the pharmacy to fill my prescriptions. They have one of those little machines by the waiting area with the cuff you stick your arm in, you know the ones? You sit down and push the green button, and it hums and squeezes your arm real tight. Anyway, it always prints out a little paper ticket, and my heart rate is always perfectly normal. Usually right around seventy beats a minute. So no, none of those things. It's really just this... this awful feeling of having to constantly be moving that I can't shake.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Worrying? Oh, no, no, it's not worry at all. It's not like I'm sitting there fretting about the bills or wondering if the roof is going to leak the next time it rains. I have a friend, Brenda, and she is a terrible worrier. She’ll sit there wringing her hands about everything under the sun, bless her heart, just working herself into an absolute state over things that haven't even happened yet. But for me, it’s not like that. My mind isn't filled with doom and gloom or anything nervous. 

It really is just this... this intense physical pull. Like my legs just want to go. I’ll be sitting down, and my mind is perfectly fine, maybe I’m just thinking about, oh, I don't know, what I'm going to make for dinner. Maybe a nice pot roast. I haven't made a pot roast in a few weeks, usually I wait for a Sunday when I have more time to let it simmer, but anyway... I'm not worried about the pot roast. I'm just thinking about it. But my body, my hands and my feet, they just feel like they absolutely have to be doing something right that very second. 

So, no, I wouldn't call it anxious thoughts. I'm not a nervous person, really. Never have been. Even when my husband was alive and he'd get all worked up over doing the taxes every April, I was always the calm one telling him it would all work out. It's just that my body won't listen to my brain when my brain says it's time to relax. It's purely a physical thing, almost like a terrible itch you can't scratch, just this constant, nagging need to get up, walk around, pick something up, put it back down... I'll literally get up from my chair, walk all the way into the kitchen, look around, and think, "Why on earth am I in here?" I'm not worried about anything in the kitchen. But my feet just carried me there because sitting still was driving me absolutely batty. It's not my mind being afraid or nervous, doctor, it's just my bones absolutely refusing to stay put.

I keep the thermostat set right at 71 degrees, which I think is perfectly reasonable, but my sister comes over and she says, \"How can you stand it in here, it's freezing!\" But I just tell her to bring a sweater. I don't sweat unless I'm out pulling weeds in the dead of July, and even then, I try to get all my yard work done before ten in the morning so I don't overheat. \n\nAnd my heart... let me think. No, no racing. Like I said before, I take my blood pressure pill every morning, and I actually check my pulse sometimes when I'm at the grocery store waiting for the pharmacy to fill my prescriptions. They have one of those little machines by the waiting area with the cuff you stick your arm in, you know the ones? You sit down and push the green button, and it hums and squeezes your arm real tight. Anyway, it always prints out a little paper ticket, and my heart rate is always perfectly normal. Usually right around seventy beats a minute. So no, none of those things. It's really just this... this awful feeling of having to constantly be moving that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this need to keep moving, do you also notice yourself worrying or having anxious thoughts running through your mind, or is it mainly just a physical urge to be in motion without any particular worry attached to it?\n\nPatient: Worrying? Oh, no, no, it's not worry at all. It's not like I'm sitting there fretting about the bills or wondering if the roof is going to leak the next time it rains. I have a friend, Brenda, and she is a terrible worrier. She’ll sit there wringing her hands about everything under the sun, bless her heart, just working herself into an absolute state over things that haven't even happened yet. But for me, it’s not like that. My mind isn't filled with doom and gloom or anything nervous. \n\nIt really is just this... this intense physical pull. Like my legs just want to go. I’ll be sitting down, and my mind is perfectly fine, maybe I’m just thinking about, oh, I don't know, what I'm going to make for dinner. Maybe a nice pot roast. I haven't made a pot roast in a few weeks, usually I wait for a Sunday when I have more time to let it simmer, but anyway... I'm not worried about the pot roast. I'm just thinking about it. But my body, my hands and my feet, they just feel like they absolutely have to be doing something right that very second. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't call it anxious thoughts. I'm not a nervous person, really. Never have been. Even when my husband was alive and he'd get all worked up over doing the taxes every April, I was always the calm one telling him it would all work out. It's just that my body won't listen to my brain when my brain says it's time to relax. It's purely a physical thing, almost like a terrible itch you can't scratch, just this constant, nagging need to get up, walk around, pick something up, put it back down... I'll literally get up from my chair, walk all the way into the kitchen, look around, and think, \"Why on earth am I in here?\" I'm not worried about anything in the kitchen. But my feet just carried me there because sitting still was driving me absolutely batty. It's not my mind being afraid or nervous, doctor, it's just my bones absolutely refusing to stay put.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

No hyperthyroid signs (weight loss, heat intolerance, tachycardia) to support a medical cause other than possible drug-induced akathisia. G25.71 remains most consistent with the described phenomenology; F32.9 retained pending mood assessment.", "is_final": false}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Down? Sad? Oh, goodness, no. I’m really not a melancholy person at all. I try to look on the bright side of things, you know? Like I always told my kids when they were growing up—every cloud has a silver lining, you just have to squint a little to see it sometimes. So no, I haven't been feeling blue or crying or anything like that. 

And as for losing interest in things... well, I still want to do all my usual things, I really do. I still love the idea of them! Like my gardening. I have a lovely little patch of hostas and daylilies out by the back fence. I still enjoy them immensely, and I still go out there with my little foam kneeling pad—it's this bright green one my daughter bought me for Mother's Day a few years back because my knees aren't quite what they used to be—and I have every intention of getting the flower beds looking absolutely perfect. 

But it's not that I've lost interest, it's just that I get out there, pull maybe two or three dandelions, and then I'll remember I left the watering can on the front porch. So I go to get that, but on the way I notice the bird feeder is empty, so I go into the garage for the sunflower seeds, and then I see the broom and think I should sweep the garage floor... and well, you get the picture. 

But the enjoyment? The pleasure of it? Oh, that's absolutely still there. I haven't lost a drop of interest in my hobbies or my friends. It's just so frustrating that I'm always buzzing around like an old refrigerator motor and I can't just settle down and focus long enough to actually finish the things I enjoy doing. But no, I'm not sad in the slightest. I'm perfectly happy in my mood, just... well, just terribly antsy and scattered, like I said before.

I have one of those little plastic pill organizers, you know the ones? With the days of the week stamped on the lids, Sunday through Saturday. I have to use it because if I just leave the bottles in the cabinet, I'll open one up, set it on the counter, get distracted by a smudge on the bathroom mirror, and completely forget if I actually swallowed the pill or just thought about swallowing it! \n\nBut as for anything new, or changing any doses... no. No, I haven't started anything new at all. Definitely nothing for my mood or for my stomach. Thank goodness I don't get nauseous, I've always had a cast-iron stomach, even when I was younger and eating all sorts of junk. I just take my regular daily vitamin, and my calcium for my bones, and the exact same blood pressure pill I’ve been on since right around the time I stopped working. That must be, oh, six or seven years ago now. \n\nBut no, nothing psychiatric or anything like that. I’m very particular about what I take, and I always make sure to read those little folded-up papers they staple to the bag at the pharmacy, even though the print is so tiny nowadays you practically need a magnifying glass just to see the side effects. It's really just this... this awful feeling of having to constantly be moving that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this need to keep moving, do you also notice yourself worrying or having anxious thoughts running through your mind, or is it mainly just a physical urge to be in motion without any particular worry attached to it?\n\nPatient: Worrying? Oh, no, no, it's not worry at all. It's not like I'm sitting there fretting about the bills or wondering if the roof is going to leak the next time it rains. I have a friend, Brenda, and she is a terrible worrier. She’ll sit there wringing her hands about everything under the sun, bless her heart, just working herself into an absolute state over things that haven't even happened yet. But for me, it’s not like that. My mind isn't filled with doom and gloom or anything nervous. \n\nIt really is just this... this intense physical pull. Like my legs just want to go. I’ll be sitting down, and my mind is perfectly fine, maybe I’m just thinking about, oh, I don't know, what I'm going to make for dinner. Maybe a nice pot roast. I haven't made a pot roast in a few weeks, usually I wait for a Sunday when I have more time to let it simmer, but anyway... I'm not worried about the pot roast. I'm just thinking about it. But my body, my hands and my feet, they just feel like they absolutely have to be doing something right that very second. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't call it anxious thoughts. I'm not a nervous person, really. Never have been. Even when my husband was alive and he'd get all worked up over doing the taxes every April, I was always the calm one telling him it would all work out. It's just that my body won't listen to my brain when my brain says it's time to relax. It's purely a physical thing, almost like a terrible itch you can't scratch, just this constant, nagging need to get up, walk around, pick something up, put it back down... I'll literally get up from my chair, walk all the way into the kitchen, look around, and think, \"Why on earth am I in here?\" I'm not worried about anything in the kitchen. But my feet just carried me there because sitting still was driving me absolutely batty. It's not my mind being afraid or nervous, doctor, it's just my bones absolutely refusing to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you noticed feeling down, sad, or losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Down? Sad? Oh, goodness, no. I’m really not a melancholy person at all. I try to look on the bright side of things, you know? Like I always told my kids when they were growing up—every cloud has a silver lining, you just have to squint a little to see it sometimes. So no, I haven't been feeling blue or crying or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... well, I still want to do all my usual things, I really do. I still love the idea of them! Like my gardening. I have a lovely little patch of hostas and daylilies out by the back fence. I still enjoy them immensely, and I still go out there with my little foam kneeling pad—it's this bright green one my daughter bought me for Mother's Day a few years back because my knees aren't quite what they used to be—and I have every intention of getting the flower beds looking absolutely perfect. \n\nBut it's not that I've lost interest, it's just that I get out there, pull maybe two or three dandelions, and then I'll remember I left the watering can on the front porch. So I go to get that, but on the way I notice the bird feeder is empty, so I go into the garage for the sunflower seeds, and then I see the broom and think I should sweep the garage floor... and well, you get the picture. \n\nBut the enjoyment? The pleasure of it? Oh, that's absolutely still there. I haven't lost a drop of interest in my hobbies or my friends. It's just so frustrating that I'm always buzzing around like an old refrigerator motor and I can't just settle down and focus long enough to actually finish the things I enjoy doing. But no, I'm not sad in the slightest. I'm perfectly happy in my mood, just... well, just terribly antsy and scattered, like I said before.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you notice that this restless, need-to-move feeling is worse in the evening or at night, and that moving or stretching your legs specifically brings some relief?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Worse in the evening? Hmm, let me ponder on that for a second. I really don't think so. I mean, it happens in the evening, certainly. Usually after supper, I like to sit down in my recliner—it's one of those big, plush La-Z-Boys, the microfiber ones, which I highly recommend by the way because they are just so easy to clean if you happen to spill a little chamomile tea on them—and I try to watch Wheel of Fortune and Jeopardy. And yes, I am absolutely fidgeting the whole time. I'll get up during the commercials, go check to make sure I locked the front door, come back, sit down, tap my foot, get up again to adjust the blinds... 

But the thing is, it's exactly the same at ten o'clock in the morning! When I'm sitting at the kitchen table having my morning coffee and trying to read the local paper, my foot is going a mile a minute under the table, and I'm popping up every two minutes to wipe down the counter or check the mail even though the mail carrier never comes before one. So I really wouldn't say it's worse at night. It's just... always there. From the minute my feet hit the floor in the morning until I finally manage to exhaust myself enough to fall asleep. It’s an all-day affair, I'm afraid.

And as for stretching my legs bringing relief... no, not at all. It's not that kind of feeling. I mean, I do stretch! My regular doctor told me a few years ago that I should do some gentle stretches for my lower back as I get older, so I do these little calf stretches against the kitchen counter while I'm waiting for the kettle to boil. But doing that doesn't make the restless feeling go away. It's not like a muscle cramp or a charley horse where you stretch it out and say, "Oh, thank goodness, that's better." 

Moving around is just what my body feels compelled to do, but it doesn't actually fix the itch, if that makes sense. I could walk two miles around the neighborhood, or spend three hours up and down a stepladder dusting the crown molding, and the very second I sit back down on the sofa, my hands are drumming on my knees and my feet are bouncing all over again. So no, stretching doesn't relieve a thing, and the time of day doesn't seem to make a lick of difference. It's just a constant motor running inside me, morning, noon, and night.

I just can't seem to find any peace or stillness, no matter how tired I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed the dose of any new medications recently, especially anything for nausea, mood, or psychiatric symptoms?\n\nPatient: Oh, medications... well, let me think about that for a second. I have to use it because if I just leave the bottles in the cabinet, I'll open one up, set it on the counter, get distracted by a smudge on the bathroom mirror, and completely forget if I actually swallowed the pill or just thought about swallowing it! \n\nBut as for anything new, or changing any doses... no. No, I haven't started anything new at all. Definitely nothing for my mood or for my stomach. Thank goodness I don't get nauseous, I've always had a cast-iron stomach, even when I was younger and eating all sorts of junk. I just take my regular daily vitamin, and my calcium for my bones, and the exact same blood pressure pill I’ve been on since right around the time I stopped working. That must be, oh, six or seven years ago now. \n\nBut no, nothing psychiatric or anything like that. I’m very particular about what I take, and I always make sure to read those little folded-up papers they staple to the bag at the pharmacy, even though the print is so tiny nowadays you practically need a magnifying glass just to see the side effects. So to answer your question, no, everything is exactly the same as it's been for years. No changes to my medicines whatsoever.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any unexplained weight loss, feeling unusually hot or sweaty, or your heart racing lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. No weight loss here, let me tell you! I honestly wish I could say yes to that one, because I've been trying to drop about ten pounds since my niece's wedding back in—oh, when was that? 2018? 2019? Somewhere around there. But no, my weight has been exactly the same for years. I still bake my own bread, you see, and I just can't resist a warm slice with a little butter right out of the oven, so the scale hasn't budged one bit. \n\nAnd as for feeling hot or sweaty... well, no, not at all. If anything, I'm the exact opposite! I'm always walking around the house in a thick cardigan. I keep the thermostat set right at 71 degrees, which I think is perfectly reasonable, but my sister comes over and she says, \"How can you stand it in here, it's freezing!\" But I just tell her to bring a sweater. I don't sweat unless I'm out pulling weeds in the dead of July, and even then, I try to get all my yard work done before ten in the morning so I don't overheat. \n\nAnd my heart... let me think. No, no racing. Like I said before, I take my blood pressure pill every morning, and I actually check my pulse sometimes when I'm at the grocery store waiting for the pharmacy to fill my prescriptions. They have one of those little machines by the waiting area with the cuff you stick your arm in, you know the ones? You sit down and push the green button, and it hums and squeezes your arm real tight. Anyway, it always prints out a little paper ticket, and my heart rate is always perfectly normal. Usually right around seventy beats a minute. So no, none of those things. It's really just this... this awful feeling of having to constantly be moving that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this need to keep moving, do you also notice yourself worrying or having anxious thoughts running through your mind, or is it mainly just a physical urge to be in motion without any particular worry attached to it?\n\nPatient: Worrying? Oh, no, no, it's not worry at all. It's not like I'm sitting there fretting about the bills or wondering if the roof is going to leak the next time it rains. I have a friend, Brenda, and she is a terrible worrier. She’ll sit there wringing her hands about everything under the sun, bless her heart, just working herself into an absolute state over things that haven't even happened yet. But for me, it’s not like that. My mind isn't filled with doom and gloom or anything nervous. \n\nIt really is just this... this intense physical pull. Like my legs just want to go. I’ll be sitting down, and my mind is perfectly fine, maybe I’m just thinking about, oh, I don't know, what I'm going to make for dinner. Maybe a nice pot roast. I haven't made a pot roast in a few weeks, usually I wait for a Sunday when I have more time to let it simmer, but anyway... I'm not worried about the pot roast. I'm just thinking about it. But my body, my hands and my feet, they just feel like they absolutely have to be doing something right that very second. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't call it anxious thoughts. I'm not a nervous person, really. Never have been. Even when my husband was alive and he'd get all worked up over doing the taxes every April, I was always the calm one telling him it would all work out. It's just that my body won't listen to my brain when my brain says it's time to relax. It's purely a physical thing, almost like a terrible itch you can't scratch, just this constant, nagging need to get up, walk around, pick something up, put it back down... I'll literally get up from my chair, walk all the way into the kitchen, look around, and think, \"Why on earth am I in here?\" I'm not worried about anything in the kitchen. But my feet just carried me there because sitting still was driving me absolutely batty. It's not my mind being afraid or nervous, doctor, it's just my bones absolutely refusing to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you noticed feeling down, sad, or losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Down? Sad? Oh, goodness, no. I’m really not a melancholy person at all. I try to look on the bright side of things, you know? Like I always told my kids when they were growing up—every cloud has a silver lining, you just have to squint a little to see it sometimes. So no, I haven't been feeling blue or crying or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... well, I still want to do all my usual things, I really do. I still love the idea of them! Like my gardening. I have a lovely little patch of hostas and daylilies out by the back fence. I still enjoy them immensely, and I still go out there with my little foam kneeling pad—it's this bright green one my daughter bought me for Mother's Day a few years back because my knees aren't quite what they used to be—and I have every intention of getting the flower beds looking absolutely perfect. \n\nBut it's not that I've lost interest, it's just that I get out there, pull maybe two or three dandelions, and then I'll remember I left the watering can on the front porch. So I go to get that, but on the way I notice the bird feeder is empty, so I go into the garage for the sunflower seeds, and then I see the broom and think I should sweep the garage floor... and well, you get the picture. \n\nBut the enjoyment? The pleasure of it? Oh, that's absolutely still there. I haven't lost a drop of interest in my hobbies or my friends. It's just so frustrating that I'm always buzzing around like an old refrigerator motor and I can't just settle down and focus long enough to actually finish the things I enjoy doing. But no, I'm not sad in the slightest. I'm perfectly happy in my mood, just... well, just terribly antsy and scattered, like I said before.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that this restless, need-to-move feeling is worse in the evening or at night, and that moving or stretching your legs specifically brings some relief?\n\nPatient: Worse in the evening? Hmm, let me ponder on that for a second. I really don't think so. I mean, it happens in the evening, certainly. Usually after supper, I like to sit down in my recliner—it's one of those big, plush La-Z-Boys, the microfiber ones, which I highly recommend by the way because they are just so easy to clean if you happen to spill a little chamomile tea on them—and I try to watch Wheel of Fortune and Jeopardy. And yes, I am absolutely fidgeting the whole time. I'll get up during the commercials, go check to make sure I locked the front door, come back, sit down, tap my foot, get up again to adjust the blinds... \n\nBut the thing is, it's exactly the same at ten o'clock in the morning! When I'm sitting at the kitchen table having my morning coffee and trying to read the local paper, my foot is going a mile a minute under the table, and I'm popping up every two minutes to wipe down the counter or check the mail even though the mail carrier never comes before one. So I really wouldn't say it's worse at night. It's just... always there. From the minute my feet hit the floor in the morning until I finally manage to exhaust myself enough to fall asleep. It’s an all-day affair, I'm afraid.\n\nAnd as for stretching my legs bringing relief... no, not at all. It's not that kind of feeling. I mean, I do stretch! My regular doctor told me a few years ago that I should do some gentle stretches for my lower back as I get older, so I do these little calf stretches against the kitchen counter while I'm waiting for the kettle to boil. But doing that doesn't make the restless feeling go away. It's not like a muscle cramp or a charley horse where you stretch it out and say, \"Oh, thank goodness, that's better.\" \n\nMoving around is just what my body feels compelled to do, but it doesn't actually fix the itch, if that makes sense. I could walk two miles around the neighborhood, or spend three hours up and down a stepladder dusting the crown molding, and the very second I sit back down on the sofa, my hands are drumming on my knees and my feet are bouncing all over again. So no, stretching doesn't relieve a thing, and the time of day doesn't seem to make a lick of difference. It's just a constant motor running inside me, morning, noon, and night.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It's just a constant motor running inside me, morning, noon, and night.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever, at any point in your life, taken an antipsychotic medication or an anti-nausea drug like metoclopramide or prochlorperazine, even if it was a long time ago?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Antipsychotic? Oh, my heavens, no. I've never taken anything like that. I mean, my brain is always going a mile a minute, as we've discussed, and I can't sit still to save my life, but I certainly haven't ever needed anything for... well, for psychosis or anything of that nature. I've always been perfectly sound of mind, just terribly restless! 

And those other names you mentioned... meto-clop-something? Pro-chloro-whatever? I can't even pronounce them, let alone tell you I've taken them! No, I really don't think so. I've always been quite lucky with my stomach, honestly. I don't get nauseous very often at all. Now, when my husband and I took a cruise up to Alaska for our anniversary years and years ago—oh, it was just beautiful, you really must go if you haven't been, the glaciers are absolutely spectacular, though it was much colder on the deck than I packed for—but anyway, the water got awfully choppy one afternoon. I did feel a tiny bit green around the gills then, but I just bought a little packet of regular over-the-counter Dramamine from the ship's gift shop and sipped on some ginger ale. Real ginger ale, mind you, with actual ginger in it, not that sugary syrup they serve at the soda fountains. The bartender was very nice about it. It settled my stomach right down in no time. 

But as for prescription anti-nausea pills? No, never. If I ever get a little stomach bug, which is rare, I just stick to plain tea and dry toast until it passes. I've never been one to run

I still bake my own bread, you see, and I just can't resist a warm slice with a little butter right out of the oven, so the scale hasn't budged one bit. \n\nAnd as for feeling hot or sweaty... well, no, not at all. No, no racing. Like I said before, I take my blood pressure pill every morning, and I actually check my pulse sometimes when I'm at the grocery store waiting for the pharmacy to fill my prescriptions. They have one of those little machines by the waiting area with the cuff you stick your arm in, you know the ones? You sit down and push the green button, and it hums and squeezes your arm real tight. Anyway, it always prints out a little paper ticket, and my heart rate is always perfectly normal. Usually right around seventy beats a minute. So no, none of those things. It's really just this... this awful feeling of having to constantly be moving that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this need to keep moving, do you also notice yourself worrying or having anxious thoughts running through your mind, or is it mainly just a physical urge to be in motion without any particular worry attached to it?\n\nPatient: Worrying? Oh, no, no, it's not worry at all. It's not like I'm sitting there fretting about the bills or wondering if the roof is going to leak the next time it rains. I have a friend, Brenda, and she is a terrible worrier. She’ll sit there wringing her hands about everything under the sun, bless her heart, just working herself into an absolute state over things that haven't even happened yet. But for me, it’s not like that. My mind isn't filled with doom and gloom or anything nervous. \n\nIt really is just this... this intense physical pull. Like my legs just want to go. I’ll be sitting down, and my mind is perfectly fine, maybe I’m just thinking about, oh, I don't know, what I'm going to make for dinner. Maybe a nice pot roast. I haven't made a pot roast in a few weeks, usually I wait for a Sunday when I have more time to let it simmer, but anyway... I'm not worried about the pot roast. I'm just thinking about it. But my body, my hands and my feet, they just feel like they absolutely have to be doing something right that very second. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't call it anxious thoughts. I'm not a nervous person, really. Never have been. Even when my husband was alive and he'd get all worked up over doing the taxes every April, I was always the calm one telling him it would all work out. It's just that my body won't listen to my brain when my brain says it's time to relax. It's purely a physical thing, almost like a terrible itch you can't scratch, just this constant, nagging need to get up, walk around, pick something up, put it back down... I'll literally get up from my chair, walk all the way into the kitchen, look around, and think, \"Why on earth am I in here?\" I'm not worried about anything in the kitchen. But my feet just carried me there because sitting still was driving me absolutely batty. Sad? Oh, goodness, no. I’m really not a melancholy person at all. I try to look on the bright side of things, you know? Like I always told my kids when they were growing up—every cloud has a silver lining, you just have to squint a little to see it sometimes. So no, I haven't been feeling blue or crying or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... well, I still want to do all my usual things, I really do. But no, I'm not sad in the slightest. I'm perfectly happy in my mood, just... well, just terribly antsy and scattered, like I said before.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that this restless, need-to-move feeling is worse in the evening or at night, and that moving or stretching your legs specifically brings some relief?\n\nPatient: Worse in the evening? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Crawling? Oh, heavens no, that sounds just awful! Like having little bugs under your skin or something. I was actually watching a program on PBS the other week—or maybe it was the Discovery channel, I can't quite recall, I was flipping back and forth while I was dusting the living room—and they were showing these insects in the rainforest, and just thinking about things crawling makes me shudder from head to toe. But no, my legs don't feel anything like that at all. There's no tingling, either. Not like when your foot falls asleep because you sat on it funny and you get those awful pins and needles. It’s nothing like that. 

And as for aching... well, I mean, I am sixty-six years old, so I certainly get the occasional normal ache and pain! If the weather is turning and it's going to rain, or if I've been standing on the hard linoleum in the kitchen for three hours making my Sunday pot roast, my knees will certainly let me know about it. I actually started wearing those supportive slip-on shoes around the house—the ones with the really thick memory foam soles, my daughter ordered them for me out of a catalog last Christmas—and they help a lot with the standing. 

But that kind of regular ache isn't what makes me want to move. The urge to move is just... it's just pure energy, really. It's like my legs have their own little battery pack and they just have to keep going. There's no pain or weird creepy-crawly feeling causing it. It’s exactly like I said, just this constant, restless buzzing where my feet just want to tap and bounce all on their own, even when I'd very much like them to just stay put for five minutes! So no, no uncomfortable sensations in the legs themselves, just the absolute inability to keep them still.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nG25.71, G25.81\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—I'm Dr. [Name]. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello, Dr. [Name]. I appreciate you taking the time to see me. It's just... well, it's been getting to the point where I don't know what else to do, you know? I came in because I feel like I just cannot settle inside my own skin anymore. It's this awful, constant feeling. Like, even when I am just absolutely worn out—and at my age, I get worn out a lot quicker than I used to, let me tell you—I still feel like I have to jump up and do the next thing. I should be sitting still, maybe watching a little television or reading a magazine, resting my bones, but my body just won't let me. It's like there's a motor humming inside me that won't switch off, and it's exhausting, it really is. I try to sit in my favorite chair, the one by the window where I can see the birds, but within two minutes, I'm thinking about the dishes, or the laundry, or something out in the yard that doesn't even need doing. I just can't seem to find any peace or stillness, no matter how tired I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed the dose of any new medications recently, especially anything for nausea, mood, or psychiatric symptoms?\n\nPatient: Oh, medications... well, let me think about that for a second. I have one of those little plastic pill organizers, you know the ones? With the days of the week stamped on the lids, Sunday through Saturday. I have to use it because if I just leave the bottles in the cabinet, I'll open one up, set it on the counter, get distracted by a smudge on the bathroom mirror, and completely forget if I actually swallowed the pill or just thought about swallowing it! \n\nBut as for anything new, or changing any doses... no. No, I haven't started anything new at all. Definitely nothing for my mood or for my stomach. Thank goodness I don't get nauseous, I've always had a cast-iron stomach, even when I was younger and eating all sorts of junk. I just take my regular daily vitamin, and my calcium for my bones, and the exact same blood pressure pill I’ve been on since right around the time I stopped working. That must be, oh, six or seven years ago now. \n\nBut no, nothing psychiatric or anything like that. I’m very particular about what I take, and I always make sure to read those little folded-up papers they staple to the bag at the pharmacy, even though the print is so tiny nowadays you practically need a magnifying glass just to see the side effects. So to answer your question, no, everything is exactly the same as it's been for years. No changes to my medicines whatsoever.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any unexplained weight loss, feeling unusually hot or sweaty, or your heart racing lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. No weight loss here, let me tell you! I honestly wish I could say yes to that one, because I've been trying to drop about ten pounds since my niece's wedding back in—oh, when was that? 2018? 2019? Somewhere around there. But no, my weight has been exactly the same for years. I still bake my own bread, you see, and I just can't resist a warm slice with a little butter right out of the oven, so the scale hasn't budged one bit. \n\nAnd as for feeling hot or sweaty... well, no, not at all. If anything, I'm the exact opposite! I'm always walking around the house in a thick cardigan. I keep the thermostat set right at 71 degrees, which I think is perfectly reasonable, but my sister comes over and she says, \"How can you stand it in here, it's freezing!\" But I just tell her to bring a sweater. I don't sweat unless I'm out pulling weeds in the dead of July, and even then, I try to get all my yard work done before ten in the morning so I don't overheat. \n\nAnd my heart... let me think. No, no racing. Like I said before, I take my blood pressure pill every morning, and I actually check my pulse sometimes when I'm at the grocery store waiting for the pharmacy to fill my prescriptions. They have one of those little machines by the waiting area with the cuff you stick your arm in, you know the ones? You sit down and push the green button, and it hums and squeezes your arm real tight. Anyway, it always prints out a little paper ticket, and my heart rate is always perfectly normal. Usually right around seventy beats a minute. So no, none of those things. It's really just this... this awful feeling of having to constantly be moving that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this need to keep moving, do you also notice yourself worrying or having anxious thoughts running through your mind, or is it mainly just a physical urge to be in motion without any particular worry attached to it?\n\nPatient: Worrying? Oh, no, no, it's not worry at all. It's not like I'm sitting there fretting about the bills or wondering if the roof is going to leak the next time it rains. I have a friend, Brenda, and she is a terrible worrier. She’ll sit there wringing her hands about everything under the sun, bless her heart, just working herself into an absolute state over things that haven't even happened yet. But for me, it’s not like that. My mind isn't filled with doom and gloom or anything nervous. \n\nIt really is just this... this intense physical pull. Like my legs just want to go. I’ll be sitting down, and my mind is perfectly fine, maybe I’m just thinking about, oh, I don't know, what I'm going to make for dinner. Maybe a nice pot roast. I haven't made a pot roast in a few weeks, usually I wait for a Sunday when I have more time to let it simmer, but anyway... I'm not worried about the pot roast. I'm just thinking about it. But my body, my hands and my feet, they just feel like they absolutely have to be doing something right that very second. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't call it anxious thoughts. I'm not a nervous person, really. Never have been. Even when my husband was alive and he'd get all worked up over doing the taxes every April, I was always the calm one telling him it would all work out. It's just that my body won't listen to my brain when my brain says it's time to relax. It's purely a physical thing, almost like a terrible itch you can't scratch, just this constant, nagging need to get up, walk around, pick something up, put it back down... I'll literally get up from my chair, walk all the way into the kitchen, look around, and think, \"Why on earth am I in here?\" I'm not worried about anything in the kitchen. But my feet just carried me there because sitting still was driving me absolutely batty. It's not my mind being afraid or nervous, doctor, it's just my bones absolutely refusing to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you noticed feeling down, sad, or losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Down? Sad? Oh, goodness, no. I’m really not a melancholy person at all. I try to look on the bright side of things, you know? Like I always told my kids when they were growing up—every cloud has a silver lining, you just have to squint a little to see it sometimes. So no, I haven't been feeling blue or crying or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... well, I still want to do all my usual things, I really do. I still love the idea of them! Like my gardening. I have a lovely little patch of hostas and daylilies out by the back fence. I still enjoy them immensely, and I still go out there with my little foam kneeling pad—it's this bright green one my daughter bought me for Mother's Day a few years back because my knees aren't quite what they used to be—and I have every intention of getting the flower beds looking absolutely perfect. \n\nBut it's not that I've lost interest, it's just that I get out there, pull maybe two or three dandelions, and then I'll remember I left the watering can on the front porch. So I go to get that, but on the way I notice the bird feeder is empty, so I go into the garage for the sunflower seeds, and then I see the broom and think I should sweep the garage floor... and well, you get the picture. \n\nBut the enjoyment? The pleasure of it? Oh, that's absolutely still there. I haven't lost a drop of interest in my hobbies or my friends. It's just so frustrating that I'm always buzzing around like an old refrigerator motor and I can't just settle down and focus long enough to actually finish the things I enjoy doing. But no, I'm not sad in the slightest. I'm perfectly happy in my mood, just... well, just terribly antsy and scattered, like I said before.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that this restless, need-to-move feeling is worse in the evening or at night, and that moving or stretching your legs specifically brings some relief?\n\nPatient: Worse in the evening? Hmm, let me ponder on that for a second. I really don't think so. I mean, it happens in the evening, certainly. Usually after supper, I like to sit down in my recliner—it's one of those big, plush La-Z-Boys, the microfiber ones, which I highly recommend by the way because they are just so easy to clean if you happen to spill a little chamomile tea on them—and I try to watch Wheel of Fortune and Jeopardy. And yes, I am absolutely fidgeting the whole time. I'll get up during the commercials, go check to make sure I locked the front door, come back, sit down, tap my foot, get up again to adjust the blinds... \n\nBut the thing is, it's exactly the same at ten o'clock in the morning! When I'm sitting at the kitchen table having my morning coffee and trying to read the local paper, my foot is going a mile a minute under the table, and I'm popping up every two minutes to wipe down the counter or check the mail even though the mail carrier never comes before one. So I really wouldn't say it's worse at night. It's just... always there. From the minute my feet hit the floor in the morning until I finally manage to exhaust myself enough to fall asleep. It’s an all-day affair, I'm afraid.\n\nAnd as for stretching my legs bringing relief... no, not at all. It's not that kind of feeling. I mean, I do stretch! My regular doctor told me a few years ago that I should do some gentle stretches for my lower back as I get older, so I do these little calf stretches against the kitchen counter while I'm waiting for the kettle to boil. But doing that doesn't make the restless feeling go away. It's not like a muscle cramp or a charley horse where you stretch it out and say, \"Oh, thank goodness, that's better.\" \n\nMoving around is just what my body feels compelled to do, but it doesn't actually fix the itch, if that makes sense. I could walk two miles around the neighborhood, or spend three hours up and down a stepladder dusting the crown molding, and the very second I sit back down on the sofa, my hands are drumming on my knees and my feet are bouncing all over again. So no, stretching doesn't relieve a thing, and the time of day doesn't seem to make a lick of difference. It's just a constant motor running inside me, morning, noon, and night.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever, at any point in your life, taken an antipsychotic medication or an anti-nausea drug like metoclopramide or prochlorperazine, even if it was a long time ago?\n\nPatient: Antipsychotic? Oh, my heavens, no. I've never taken anything like that. I mean, my brain is always going a mile a minute, as we've discussed, and I can't sit still to save my life, but I certainly haven't ever needed anything for... well, for psychosis or anything of that nature. I've always been perfectly sound of mind, just terribly restless! \n\nAnd those other names you mentioned... meto-clop-something? Pro-chloro-whatever? I can't even pronounce them, let alone tell you I've taken them! No, I really don't think so. I've always been quite lucky with my stomach, honestly. I don't get nauseous very often at all. Now, when my husband and I took a cruise up to Alaska for our anniversary years and years ago—oh, it was just beautiful, you really must go if you haven't been, the glaciers are absolutely spectacular, though it was much colder on the deck than I packed for—but anyway, the water got awfully choppy one afternoon. I did feel a tiny bit green around the gills then, but I just bought a little packet of regular over-the-counter Dramamine from the ship's gift shop and sipped on some ginger ale. Real ginger ale, mind you, with actual ginger in it, not that sugary syrup they serve at the soda fountains. The bartender was very nice about it. It settled my stomach right down in no time. \n\nBut as for prescription anti-nausea pills? No, never. If I ever get a little stomach bug, which is rare, I just stick to plain tea and dry toast until it passes. I've never been one to run\n\nDoctor: Aside from the general urge to move, do you notice any specific uncomfortable sensations—like crawling, tingling, or aching—located in your legs themselves?\n\nPatient: Crawling? Oh, heavens no, that sounds just awful! Like having little bugs under your skin or something. I was actually watching a program on PBS the other week—or maybe it was the Discovery channel, I can't quite recall, I was flipping back and forth while I was dusting the living room—and they were showing these insects in the rainforest, and just thinking about things crawling makes me shudder from head to toe. But no, my legs don't feel anything like that at all. There's no tingling, either. Not like when your foot falls asleep because you sat on it funny and you get those awful pins and needles. It’s nothing like that. \n\nAnd as for aching... well, I mean, I am sixty-six years old, so I certainly get the occasional normal ache and pain! If the weather is turning and it's going to rain, or if I've been standing on the hard linoleum in the kitchen for three hours making my Sunday pot roast, my knees will certainly let me know about it. I actually started wearing those supportive slip-on shoes around the house—the ones with the really thick memory foam soles, my daughter ordered them for me out of a catalog last Christmas—and they help a lot with the standing. \n\nBut that kind of regular ache isn't what makes me want to move. The urge to move is just... it's just pure energy, really. It's like my legs have their own little battery pack and they just have to keep going. There's no pain or weird creepy-crawly feeling causing it. It’s exactly like I said, just this constant, restless buzzing where my feet just want to tap and bounce all on their own, even when I'd very much like them to just stay put for five minutes! So no, no uncomfortable sensations in the legs themselves, just the absolute inability to keep them still.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n[S013] Quiet_Activity_Difficulty: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n[S014] Constant_Activity: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n[S015] Excessive_Talking: Often talks excessively.\n[S016] Blurting_Answers: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n[S017] Waiting_Turn_Difficulty: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n[S018] Interrupting_Intruding: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n[S019] Delusions: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.  Subtypes: Persecutory Delusions, Referential Delusions, Grandiose Delusions, Erotomanic Delusions, Nihilistic Delusions, Somatic Delusions, Jealous Delusions\n[S020] Hallucinations: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.  Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. It's this awful, constant feeling. Like, even when I am just absolutely worn out—and at my age, I get worn out a lot quicker than I used to, let me tell you—I still feel like I have to jump up and do the next thing. I should be sitting still, maybe watching a little television or reading a magazine, resting my bones, but my body just won't let me. It's like there's a motor humming inside me that won't switch off, and it's exhausting, it really is. Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nOh, medications... well, let me think about that for a second. I have one of those little plastic pill organizers, you know the ones? With the days of the week stamped on the lids, Sunday through Saturday. I have to use it because if I just leave the bottles in the cabinet, I'll open one up, set it on the counter, get distracted by a smudge on the bathroom mirror, and completely forget if I actually swallowed the pill or just thought about swallowing it! \n\nBut as for anything new, or changing any doses... no. No, I haven't started anything new at all. Definitely nothing for my mood or for my stomach. Thank goodness I don't get nauseous, I've always had a cast-iron stomach, even when I was younger and eating all sorts of junk. I just take my regular daily vitamin, and my calcium for my bones, and the exact same blood pressure pill I’ve been on since right around the time I stopped working. That must be, oh, six or seven years ago now. \n\nBut no, nothing psychiatric or anything like that. I’m very particular about what I take, and I always make sure to read those little folded-up papers they staple to the bag at the pharmacy, even though the print is so tiny nowadays you practically need a magnifying glass just to see the side effects. So to answer your question, no, everything is exactly the same as it's been for years. Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.
